Practice questions

Original questions written by HomiLabs in the style of the exam, with answers and mark schemes. They are not Cambridge past paper questions and have not been reviewed by Cambridge. Try each one before opening the answer.

Q1Planning: method & apparatus · Core · Paper 5 style · 6 marks
  1. (a) A student plans to investigate how temperature affects the rate of the reaction between sodium thiosulfate and hydrochloric acid by timing how long a cross under the flask takes to disappear. List the apparatus needed. [3]
  2. (b) Describe how the student should bring the solutions to each temperature and when to start and stop the clock. [2]
  3. (c) Suggest how many temperatures should be used and over what range. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Conical flask; measuring cylinders (or burettes) for each solution; thermometer; water bath (or kettle/ice to set temperature); stopwatch; paper with a cross.
    1 mark for each pair: flask + measuring cylinders; thermometer + water bath; stopwatch + marked cross.
  2. (b) Warm both solutions separately in the water bath to the required temperature before mixing (record temperature of mixture); start the clock as the acid is added; stop when the cross can no longer be seen from above.
    B1 heat separately before mixing; B1 start on mixing, stop when cross disappears.
  3. (c) At least five temperatures, e.g. 20–60 °C.
    B1.
Q2Planning: method & apparatus · Core · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

A student in Karachi wants to find the concentration of ethanoic acid in a bottle of white vinegar, which is expected to be about 0.85 mol/dm³. The only alkali available is 0.100 mol/dm³ sodium hydroxide.

  1. (a) Calculate the volume of 0.100 mol/dm³ NaOH needed to neutralise 25.0 cm³ of undiluted vinegar, and use your answer to explain why the vinegar must be diluted first. [2]
  2. (b) Describe how the student should dilute the vinegar exactly ten times, naming the apparatus used. [2]
  3. (c) Name a suitable indicator, give its colour change at the end point and explain why methyl orange is not suitable. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 212.5 cm³; far more than one 50 cm³ burette can deliver, so the vinegar is diluted (e.g. 1 in 10) to give a titre of about 21 cm³.
    M1 0.85 × 25.0 ÷ 0.100 = 212.5 cm³; A1 too big for one burette filling / dilute ×10 to give ≈ 21 cm³.
  2. (b) Pipette 25.0 cm³ of vinegar into a 250.0 cm³ volumetric flask, make up to the mark with distilled water and invert to mix.
    B1 25.0 cm³ pipette + 250.0 cm³ volumetric flask; B1 distilled water to the mark (bottom of meniscus) and invert/shake to mix.
  3. (c) Phenolphthalein, colourless to (pale) pink. A weak acid–strong base titration has an end point pH of about 8–9, outside the range of methyl orange (about 3–4.5).
    B1 phenolphthalein colourless → pink; B1 reason about end-point pH / sharp pH change in alkaline region.
Q3Planning: method & apparatus · Core · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

Oxygen is made by adding a small amount of manganese(IV) oxide to 20.0 cm³ of 0.200 mol/dm³ hydrogen peroxide.2H₂O₂ → 2H₂O + O₂Molar gas volume at room conditions = 24.0 dm³/mol.

  1. (a) Calculate the maximum volume of oxygen, in cm³, that can be produced at room conditions. [2]
  2. (b) Two pieces of apparatus are available to collect the gas: a 100 cm³ gas syringe and a 25 cm³ measuring cylinder over water. Choose the better one and explain your choice. [2]
  3. (c) Suggest how the reaction can be started without losing any oxygen before the bung is in place. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 48.0 cm³
    M1 n(H₂O₂) = 4.00 × 10⁻³ mol, n(O₂) = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol; A1 × 24 000 = 48.0 cm³.
  2. (b) The 100 cm³ gas syringe: its capacity is larger than 48 cm³, whereas the 25 cm³ cylinder would overflow.
    B1 gas syringe; B1 capacity argument using the answer to (a).
  3. (c) Place the MnO₂ in a small tube (or tied in a muslin bag) inside the sealed flask, then tip/shake it into the peroxide after the apparatus is connected.
    B1 any method that mixes reactants only after the system is sealed.
Q4Planning: method & apparatus · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

A student plans to measure the temperature change when 5.35 g of ammonium chloride (Mr 53.5) dissolves in 50.0 cm³ of water in a polystyrene cup. ΔH(solution) of NH₄Cl is +14.8 kJ/mol. Assume 50.0 g of solution with specific heat capacity 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹.

  1. (a) Calculate the expected temperature change and state whether the temperature rises or falls. [2]
  2. (b) Name the apparatus used to measure the 50.0 cm³ of water and the 5.35 g of solid. [1]
  3. (c) Give two reasons for using a polystyrene cup rather than a glass beaker. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Fall of 7.08 °C
    M1 n = 0.100 mol, q = 1480 J; A1 ΔT = 1480 ÷ (50.0 × 4.18) = 7.08 K, a fall.
  2. (b) A 50 cm³ measuring cylinder (or burette/pipette) and a balance reading to 0.01 g.
    B1 both.
  3. (c) Polystyrene is a good insulator so less heat is gained from the surroundings; it has a very low heat capacity so it absorbs little energy itself.
    1 mark each.
Q5Planning: method & apparatus · Core · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

A laboratory has these measuring devices: 100 cm³ beaker; 50 cm³ measuring cylinder; 25.0 cm³ volumetric pipette; 50.00 cm³ burette; 250.0 cm³ volumetric flask.

  1. (a) Choose the most suitable device for each task.(i) Transferring exactly 25.0 cm³ of sodium hydroxide into a conical flask for titration.(ii) Adding about 20 cm³ of dilute sulfuric acid, which is in excess, to a solid.(iii) Adding 3.00, 6.00, 9.00, 12.00 and 15.00 cm³ of acid to five separate tubes.(iv) Making up a standard solution. [4]
  2. (b) Explain why a beaker should never be used to measure a volume in a quantitative experiment. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) (i) volumetric pipette; (ii) measuring cylinder; (iii) burette; (iv) volumetric flask.
    1 mark each.
  2. (b) Beaker graduations are only approximate (large uncertainty, about ±5 %).
    B1.
Q6Planning: method & apparatus · Standard · Paper 5 style · 7 marks
  1. (a) Plan an experiment to find x in hydrated magnesium sulfate, MgSO₄·xH₂O, by heating. Your plan should include the apparatus, the measurements to be taken, and how you will make sure all the water has been removed. [6]
  2. (b) Suggest why a lid is placed loosely on the crucible during heating. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Weigh an empty crucible (and lid); add about 2–3 g of the hydrated salt and reweigh; heat gently at first then strongly with a Bunsen burner on a pipe-clay triangle; cool (in a desiccator) and weigh; heat to constant mass (repeat heating, cooling and weighing until two masses agree within 0.01 g); calculate mass of water and of anhydrous MgSO₄, convert to moles and find the ratio.
    1 mark each for: crucible on pipe-clay triangle / tripod / Bunsen; weigh empty crucible; weigh crucible + hydrated salt (suitable mass, 1–5 g); heat, cool, weigh; heat to constant mass described; processing: moles water ÷ moles MgSO₄ = x.
  2. (b) To prevent loss of solid by spitting, while letting water vapour escape.
    B1.
Q7Planning: method & apparatus · Standard · Paper 5 style · 7 marks

A student in Lahore plans to find the solubility of calcium hydroxide in water at 25 °C. Excess solid Ca(OH)₂ is shaken with distilled water and left in a water bath at 25 °C. The mixture is filtered and 25.0 cm³ of the filtrate is titrated with 0.0500 mol/dm³ hydrochloric acid; the mean titre is 23.30 cm³.Ca(OH)₂ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + 2H₂O (Mr Ca(OH)₂ = 74.1)

  1. (a) Explain why excess solid is used and why the mixture is left in the water bath for some time before filtering. [2]
  2. (b) State why the filter paper, funnel and collecting flask must be dry, and why any solid in the filtrate must be avoided. [2]
  3. (c) Calculate the solubility of calcium hydroxide in mol/dm³ and in g/dm³, to 3 significant figures. [3]
Show answer
  1. (a) Excess solid ensures the solution is saturated; time at 25 °C lets the solution reach equilibrium (saturation) at the stated temperature.
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Water would dilute the saturated solution, lowering the titre; solid Ca(OH)₂ in the filtrate would react with acid and make the titre too high.
    B1 each.
  3. (c) 0.0233 mol/dm³; 1.73 g/dm³
    M1 n(HCl) = 1.165 × 10⁻³; M1 n(Ca(OH)₂) = 5.825 × 10⁻⁴ in 25.0 cm³; A1 0.0233 mol/dm³ and 1.73 g/dm³.

Q9Planning: method & apparatus · Standard · Paper 5 style · 7 marks

Hydrogen peroxide oxidises iodide ions in acid: H₂O₂ + 2I⁻ + 2H⁺ → I₂ + 2H₂O. A student plans an 'iodine clock' to find the order with respect to I⁻. Each mixture contains fixed volumes of H₂O₂, H₂SO₄, a small fixed amount of sodium thiosulfate and starch. The volume of 0.100 mol/dm³ KI is varied.

  1. (a) Five experiments use 10.0, 8.0, 6.0, 4.0 and 2.0 cm³ of KI solution. State what must be added to each mixture and in what volumes, and explain why. [2]
  2. (b) State what is timed and how a value proportional to the initial rate is obtained. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why the amount of thiosulfate must be both small and the same in each experiment. [2]
  4. (d) Describe the graph that would show the reaction is first order with respect to I⁻. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Distilled water: 0.0, 2.0, 4.0, 6.0, 8.0 cm³, so the total volume is the same in each and [I⁻] is proportional to the volume of KI.
    B1 water volumes; B1 total volume constant so concentration ∝ volume.
  2. (b) Time t from mixing until the blue-black colour appears; rate ∝ 1/t.
    B1 each.
  3. (c) Small: only a small fraction of reactant is used, so the rate measured is the initial rate; same: the same amount of I₂ is formed before the colour appears, so 1/t is proportional to rate in every experiment.
    B1 each.
  4. (d) A straight line through the origin for 1/t against volume (or concentration) of KI.
    B1.

The relative molecular mass of a volatile liquid is found by injecting 0.120 g of it into a gas syringe held in an oven at 100 °C. After all the liquid has vaporised, the volume of vapour is 66.2 cm³. Pressure = 101 kPa; R = 8.31 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.

  1. (a) Calculate the amount of vapour and the Mr of the liquid. [3]

  3. (c) Give two practical points the student must take care with to obtain an accurate result. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) n = 0.00216 mol; Mr = 55.6
    M1 conversion to Pa, m³ and K; M1 n = pV/RT = 2.16 × 10⁻³ mol; A1 Mr = 55.6.
  2. (b) Propanone
    B1 closest value.
  3. (c) Any two: read the syringe volume only when it is constant (all liquid vaporised); subtract any air already in the syringe; weigh the hypodermic syringe before and after injection to find the mass injected; oven temperature must be above the boiling point and constant.

Q11Planning: method & apparatus · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 8 marks

A student in Peshawar plans to find the percentage of calcium carbonate in hen eggshells. 1.20 g of dried, crushed eggshell is added to 50.0 cm³ of 0.500 mol/dm³ HCl (an excess). When fizzing stops, the remaining acid is titrated with 0.100 mol/dm³ NaOH; the mean titre is 22.20 cm³.CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂

  1. (a) Explain why a back titration is used rather than titrating the eggshell directly with acid. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage by mass of calcium carbonate in the eggshell. (Mr CaCO₃ = 100.1) [4]
  3. (c) Give two practical steps that would make the result more accurate. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Eggshell is an insoluble solid that reacts slowly; the end-point would be difficult to see. Adding a known excess of acid lets the reaction go to completion, then the remaining acid can be titrated accurately.
    B1 solid insoluble / slow reaction; B1 known excess reacts fully, remaining acid titrated easily.
  2. (b) 95.0 %
    M1 n(HCl) added = 0.0500 × 0.500 = 0.0250; M1 n(HCl) excess = n(NaOH) = 0.00222; M1 n(HCl) reacted = 0.02278 → n(CaCO₃) = 0.01139; A1 × 100.1 ÷ 1.20 × 100 = 95.0 %.
  3. (c) Grind/crush the eggshell and wash and dry it (remove membrane); warm the mixture or wait until fizzing stops to ensure complete reaction; repeat titrations to concordant titres; use a suitable indicator such as methyl orange or phenolphthalein.
    1 mark each for any two.

Show answer

Q13Planning: method & apparatus · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 7 marks

The enthalpy change for MgCO₃(s) → MgO(s) + CO₂(g) cannot be measured directly, so a student plans two experiments with excess 2.0 mol/dm³ HCl (50.0 cm³ each, in a polystyrene cup).Experiment A: 1.00 g MgO; temperature rise 17.5 °C.Experiment B: 2.00 g MgCO₃; temperature rise 5.5 °C.Use 50.0 g of solution, c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹; Mr MgO = 40.3, MgCO₃ = 84.3.

  2. (b) Calculate ΔH for the reaction in experiment A and in experiment B, in kJ/mol. [3]
  3. (c) Use Hess's law to calculate ΔH for the decomposition of MgCO₃. [2]
  4. (d) Suggest why experiment B has a larger percentage error from heat losses than experiment A. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Decomposition needs continuous strong heating, so the heat taken in cannot be measured / the reaction does not go to completion quickly at room temperature.
    B1.
  2. (b) A: −147 kJ/mol; B: −48.5 kJ/mol
    M1 q values 3657 J and 1150 J; M1 moles 0.02481 and 0.02372; A1 both with negative sign.

Q14Planning: method & apparatus · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 7 marks

A student plans to find Kc for CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH ⇌ CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O. 0.100 mol of ethanoic acid, 0.100 mol of ethanol and 1.00 cm³ of concentrated HCl (catalyst) are sealed in a flask and kept at 25 °C for one week. The mixture is then poured into ice-cold water and titrated with 1.00 mol/dm³ NaOH; titre 42.4 cm³. A separate titration of 1.00 cm³ of the concentrated HCl alone, diluted the same way, needs 12.0 cm³ of the NaOH (concentrated HCl is about 12 mol/dm³).

  1. (a) Explain why the flask is sealed and left for a week. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why the mixture is poured into ice-cold water before titrating. [1]
  3. (c) Calculate the amount of ethanoic acid at equilibrium, then Kc. Explain why Kc has no units here. [4]
Show answer
  1. (a) Sealed so no volatile reactants/products escape; a week because the reaction is slow and must reach equilibrium.
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Cooling and dilution slow the reaction so the equilibrium position does not shift appreciably during the titration.
    B1.
  3. (c) n(CH₃COOH) = 0.0304 mol; Kc = 5.24; no units because the moles (and volumes) cancel.
    M1 subtract HCl titre: 42.4 − 12.0 = 30.4 cm³; M1 0.0304 mol acid; M1 ester = water = 0.0696 mol, ethanol = 0.0304 mol; A1 Kc = 5.24, no units.

Q16Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks
  1. (a) In an experiment to measure an enthalpy change of neutralisation, the reactants are mixed in an uncovered glass beaker. Explain why the calculated value of the enthalpy change is less exothermic than the true value. [2]
  2. (b) Suggest two improvements. [2]
  3. (c) The student repeats the experiment three times and gets similar results. Explain why this improves reliability but not accuracy if the same apparatus is used. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Heat is lost to the surroundings (through the glass, which is a good conductor, and from the open top), so the measured temperature rise is too small.
    B1 heat loss; B1 temperature rise too small → magnitude too small.
  2. (b) Use an insulated (polystyrene) cup; fit a lid; take temperature readings over time and extrapolate back to the time of mixing.
    1 mark each for any two.
  3. (c) Repeats reduce random error/show consistency, but a systematic error such as heat loss is present every time.
    B1.
Q17Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

A student titrates 25.0 cm³ of sodium hydroxide solution with hydrochloric acid from a burette. Before starting, the student rinses every piece of glassware with distilled water only.

  1. (a) State and explain the effect on the titre of rinsing the burette with water only before filling it with acid. [2]
  2. (b) State and explain the effect on the titre of rinsing the pipette with water only before taking the alkali. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why rinsing the conical flask with distilled water has no effect on the titre. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Titre is too large: water left in the burette dilutes the acid, so more acid solution is needed.
    B1 larger; B1 acid diluted.
  2. (b) Titre is too small: the alkali is diluted, so fewer moles of NaOH are in the 25.0 cm³ portion.
    B1 smaller; B1 fewer moles of alkali.
  3. (c) The number of moles of NaOH pipetted into the flask is unchanged by extra water.
    B1.
Q18Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Core · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

A student heats copper(II) carbonate in an open crucible for five minutes, lets it cool, weighs it once and calculates the mass of carbon dioxide lost.CuCO₃ → CuO + CO₂

  1. (a) Explain why heating for a fixed time and weighing only once is a source of error, and state its effect on the calculated mass of CO₂. [2]
  2. (b) Describe the improvement. [2]
  3. (c) During heating some powder spits out of the crucible. State the effect on the calculated mass of CO₂ and suggest how to prevent it. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Decomposition may be incomplete, so the mass lost (CO₂) is too small.
    B1 incomplete decomposition; B1 mass of CO₂ too low.
  2. (b) Heat, cool (in a desiccator), weigh; repeat heating, cooling and weighing until two consecutive masses agree (constant mass).
    B1 repeat heat-cool-weigh; B1 until constant mass.
  3. (c) Mass of CO₂ too high; heat gently at first / use a lid placed slightly ajar.
    B1 too high; B1 improvement.

Q20Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

To measure the enthalpy change of Zn(s) + CuSO₄(aq) → ZnSO₄(aq) + Cu(s), a student records the temperature of the CuSO₄ solution, adds excess zinc powder, stirs, and records the temperature again after exactly 2 minutes.

Q28Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 7 marks

To find Ea for a reaction, a student measures the time t for a fixed amount of product to form at only two temperatures: 84 s at 25 °C and 22 s at 45 °C. Rate ∝ 1/t. During the 45 °C run the student notices the mixture cooled to 41 °C by the end. R = 8.31 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹; ln(k₂/k₁) = (Ea/R)(1/T₁ − 1/T₂).

  1. (a) Calculate Ea from the student's two results, in kJ/mol. [3]
  2. (b) State and explain whether the cooling during the 45 °C run makes the calculated Ea too high or too low. [2]
  3. (c) Suggest two improvements to the plan. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 52.8 kJ/mol
    M1 ln(84/22) = 1.340; M1 1/298 − 1/318 = 2.11 × 10⁻⁴; A1 52.8 kJ/mol.
  2. (b) Too low: the reaction ran at a lower average temperature, so it was slower and t too long; the rate ratio used with T₂ = 318 K is too small.
    B1 too low; B1 reasoning.
  3. (c) Use at least five temperatures and plot ln(1/t) against 1/T, Ea from the gradient; use a thermostatically controlled water bath / pre-heat both solutions; record the temperature at the start and end and use the mean.
    1 mark each.
Q29Evaluation: errors, reliability & improvements · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

Standard copper(II) sulfate solutions (0 to 0.100 mol/dm³) give a straight calibration line of absorbance against concentration through the origin with gradient 9.80 dm³/mol; the 0.100 mol/dm³ standard gave absorbance 0.98. An unknown solution gives absorbance 1.45, and the student extrapolates the line to obtain its concentration.

  1. (a) Calculate the concentration the student obtains by extrapolation. [1]
  2. (b) Explain why this value is unreliable. [2]
  3. (c) The student instead dilutes 25.0 cm³ of the unknown to 50.0 cm³ with a pipette and volumetric flask; the diluted solution has absorbance 0.72. Calculate the concentration of the original unknown. [2]
  4. (d) Give one other source of error in colorimetry and how to avoid it. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 0.148 mol/dm³
    B1.
  2. (b) It is outside the range of the standards; there is no evidence the line stays straight (absorbance may not be proportional to concentration at high absorbance).
    B1 outside range; B1 linearity not shown.
  3. (c) 0.147 mol/dm³
    M1 0.72 ÷ 9.80 = 0.0735; A1 × 2 = 0.147 mol/dm³.
  4. (d) Fingerprints/scratches/bubbles on the cuvette → handle by frosted sides, wipe clean, tap out bubbles; or wrong filter → use the complementary (red/orange) filter for blue Cu²⁺.
    B1.

Q31Planning: preparing standard solutions & dilutions · Core · Paper 5 style · 6 marks
  1. (a) Calculate the mass of anhydrous sodium carbonate needed to make 250.0 cm³ of 0.100 mol/dm³ solution. (Mr Na₂CO₃ = 106.0) [2]
  2. (b) Describe how to prepare the solution accurately. [4]
Show answer
  1. (a) 2.65 g
    M1 n = 0.100 × 0.2500 = 0.0250 mol; A1 × 106.0 = 2.65 g.
  2. (b) Weigh the solid in a weighing bottle (by difference); dissolve in a small volume of distilled water in a beaker; transfer to a 250 cm³ volumetric flask with washings from the beaker and glass rod; make up to the mark with distilled water so the bottom of the meniscus is on the line; stopper and invert several times to mix.
    1 mark each for any four: weigh by difference; dissolve in beaker; transfer with rinsings; volumetric flask; fill to the mark at eye level; invert to mix.

Q35Planning: preparing standard solutions & dilutions · Core · Paper 5 style · 4 marks

Q48Graph plotting & line of best fit · Core · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

A teacher marks a student's graph of the mass of product against the mass of reactant. The teacher notes:• points are plotted as large dots about 3 mm across• consecutive points are joined with straight lines• the y-axis is labelled 'mass' only• the x-axis scale goes 0, 3, 6, 9, 12• the line is forced through (0, 0) although the student's data show a clear intercept on the y-axis

  1. (a) Explain what is wrong with each feature and how it should be corrected. [5]
Show answer
  1. (a) Large dots → use small neat crosses or dots with circles so the centre is exact; dot-to-dot → one best-fit straight line or smooth curve; label lacks unit → 'mass of product / g'; awkward scale → use 1, 2, 5 or 10 per square; forcing through origin → draw the best-fit line through the data, only through the origin if (0,0) is a genuine data point/expected.
    1 mark per feature.

  3. (c) Explain the trend in terms of intermolecular forces. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Smooth curve: the rises are 73, 47, 41, 37, 33, 29 °C – they get smaller, so the gradient decreases.
    M1 differences; A1 curve.
  2. (b) About 124 °C (next difference about 26 °C); accept 122–128 °C.
    B1.
  3. (c) More electrons/larger surface area → stronger instantaneous dipole–induced dipole forces → more energy to separate molecules; each extra CH₂ adds a smaller fraction to the total.
    B1 id-id forces increase; B1 smaller fractional increase.

Rate constants for a reaction at four temperatures:T / K: 293, 303, 313, 323k / s⁻¹: 0.0121, 0.0238, 0.0448, 0.0813R = 8.31 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹; ln k = −Ea/(RT) + ln A

Q59Graph plotting & line of best fit · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

The concentration of a reactant B is measured over time in a first-order reaction.t / s: 0, 30, 60, 90, 120[B] / mol dm⁻³: 0.0800, 0.0567, 0.0401, 0.0284, 0.0201

  1. (a) State what should be plotted against t to give a straight line, and why. [2]
  2. (b) Using the first and last points, calculate k. [2]
  3. (c) Calculate the half-life and check it against the data. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) ln[B]: for a first-order reaction ln[B] = ln[B]₀ − kt, so the graph is a straight line of gradient −k.
    B1 ln[B]; B1 reason.
  2. (b) 0.0115 s⁻¹
    M1 gradient = (ln 0.0201 − ln 0.0800) ÷ 120; A1 k.
  3. (c) 60.2 s; [B] halves from 0.0800 to 0.0401 in 60 s, consistent.
    B1 t½ = 0.693/k; B1 check.

Q73Data processing (tables, sig. figs) · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

0.0485 g of magnesium reacts with excess acid to give 48.6 cm³ of hydrogen at room conditions (molar volume 24.0 dm³). Student X rounds n(H₂) to 2.0 × 10⁻³ mol before the next step; student Y keeps all figures.

  1. (a) Calculate the Ar of Mg obtained by each student, to 4 significant figures for comparison. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why X's method is wrong even though X's answer is closer to the accepted value 24.3. [2]
  3. (c) Comment on Y's answer. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) X: 24.25; Y: 23.95
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Rounding an intermediate value to 2 s.f. introduces an extra error (here about 1 %) unrelated to the data; the agreement is accidental. Intermediate values should be kept unrounded and only the final answer given to 3 s.f.
    B1 rounding error; B1 keep figures until end.
  3. (c) It is about 1.4 % low, so the gas volume was too large for the mass used: e.g. the gas was warmer than room temperature when read (reaction is exothermic), it contained water vapour, or the volume of acid added pushed extra air into the collector.
    B1 % low with a reason that makes the volume too large.

Q81Titration: recording, accuracy & mean titre · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

State and explain the effect of each mistake on the titre measured.

  1. (a) An air bubble in the burette jet is expelled during the titration. [1]
  2. (b) The funnel used to fill the burette is left in the top and drips into the burette during the titration. [1]
  3. (c) The student reads both burette readings from above eye level. [1]
  4. (d) The pipette is not rinsed with the solution it is used for and contains drops of water. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Titre too large: the volume of the bubble is read as liquid delivered.
    B1.
  2. (b) Titre too small: extra liquid drips into the burette during the run, so the final reading is higher than it would be and the apparent volume delivered is smaller.
    B1.
  3. (c) Little or no effect on the titre: both readings are wrong in the same way, so the difference is roughly unchanged.
    B1.
  4. (d) Titre too small: the pipetted solution is diluted, so it contains fewer moles.
    B1.

Q84Titration: recording, accuracy & mean titre · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

An iodine solution is titrated with 0.100 mol/dm³ sodium thiosulfate. 25.0 cm³ of the iodine solution needs a mean titre of 21.60 cm³.I₂ + 2S₂O₃²⁻ → 2I⁻ + S₄O₆²⁻

  1. (a) Describe when starch is added and the colour change at the end point. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why starch is not added at the start. [1]
  3. (c) Calculate the concentration of iodine. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Add starch when the solution is pale yellow (near the end point); end point is blue-black to colourless.
    B1 when; B1 colour change.
  2. (b) With a lot of iodine the starch–iodine complex forms strongly and releases iodine slowly, making the end point hard to judge (late).
    B1.
  3. (c) 0.0432 mol/dm³
    M1 n(S₂O₃²⁻) = 2.16 × 10⁻³; A1 ÷ 2 ÷ 0.0250.

    B1 two readings, ±0.10 cm³.
  2. (b) 0.41 %
    B1.

    B1 Mn²⁺ catalyst; B1 formed as product.

Q90Titration: recording, accuracy & mean titre · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

25.0 cm³ of 0.0500 mol/dm³ Na₂CO₃ is titrated with 0.100 mol/dm³ HCl. The expected titre with methyl orange is 25.00 cm³. A student uses phenolphthalein instead and gets 12.50 cm³.

  1. (a) Write equations for the reactions occurring up to each end point. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why the phenolphthalein titre is half the methyl orange titre. [2]
  3. (c) State which indicator gives the more precise result, with a reason. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Phenolphthalein: CO₃²⁻ + H⁺ → HCO₃⁻; methyl orange: CO₃²⁻ + 2H⁺ → CO₂ + H₂O (via HCO₃⁻ + H⁺ → CO₂ + H₂O).
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Phenolphthalein changes at pH ≈ 8–9, reached when all carbonate is converted to hydrogencarbonate (1 mol H⁺ per mol); methyl orange changes at pH ≈ 4, after the second protonation (2 mol H⁺).
    B1 pH ranges; B1 1 : 1 vs 1 : 2.
  3. (c) Methyl orange: the pH change at the second end point is sharper (and the titre is larger, smaller % uncertainty).
    B1.

  2. (b) Damp red litmus paper turns blue.
    B1.

Q93Qualitative analysis: cations, anions & gases · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

Halide ions are tested with aqueous silver nitrate.

  1. (a) Explain why the solution is first acidified with dilute nitric acid, and why hydrochloric acid must not be used. [2]
  2. (b) Give the colour of the precipitate with chloride, bromide and iodide, and the effect of adding aqueous ammonia to each. [3]
Show answer
  1. (a) Nitric acid removes carbonate (and other) ions that would also give a precipitate with Ag⁺; HCl would add chloride ions, giving a white precipitate whatever the sample.
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Chloride: white, soluble in NH₃(aq); bromide: cream, partially soluble in NH₃(aq); iodide: yellow, insoluble in NH₃(aq).
    1 mark per ion.

Q98Qualitative analysis: cations, anions & gases · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

A student has solutions of magnesium chloride and calcium chloride of similar, fairly low concentration.

  1. (a) Describe what is seen when NH₃(aq) is added to each. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why NaOH(aq) is less useful for telling them apart. [2]
  3. (c) Suggest why Mg²⁺ gives a precipitate with NH₃(aq) but Ca²⁺ does not. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Mg²⁺: white precipitate, insoluble in excess; Ca²⁺: no precipitate.
    B1 each.
  2. (b) Both can give white precipitates insoluble in excess (Ca(OH)₂ only at higher concentration), so the results may look the same.
    B1 both white ppts; B1 Ca²⁺ depends on concentration.
  3. (c) NH₃(aq) gives only a low OH⁻ concentration; Mg(OH)₂ is much less soluble than Ca(OH)₂, so only Mg(OH)₂ precipitates.
    B1.
Q99Qualitative analysis: cations, anions & gases · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

A solution is tested for nitrate ions by warming it with aqueous sodium hydroxide and aluminium foil.

  1. (a) State the positive result and name the gas formed. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why the solution must first be warmed with NaOH(aq) alone until no more gas is given off if ammonium ions may be present. [2]
  3. (c) Describe how nitrite could be distinguished from nitrate. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Gas that turns damp red litmus blue; ammonia.
    B1 each.
  2. (b) NH₄⁺ also gives ammonia with NaOH; it must be removed first so any ammonia then formed comes from nitrate (or nitrite) reduction.
    B1 NH₄⁺ interferes; B1 remove first.
  3. (c) Add dilute acid: nitrite gives a colourless gas (NO) that turns pale brown in air; nitrate gives no gas.
    B1.
Q100Qualitative analysis: cations, anions & gases · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

Salt Z is a pale green solid.Test 1: NaOH(aq) → green precipitate, insoluble in excess; the precipitate slowly turns brown at the surface.Test 2: dilute HNO₃ then Ba(NO₃)₂(aq) → white precipitate, insoluble in excess acid.

  2. (b) No precipitate with NaOH(aq), so no cation from the list except possibly a group 1 / Ba²⁺; W contains NH₄⁺ which accounts for the cation.

Q120Anomalous results · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

Three accurate titres are 25.15, 25.35 and 25.25 cm³. The student's rule is that titres used for the mean must agree within 0.10 cm³. Each burette reading has an uncertainty of ±0.05 cm³.

  1. (a) Explain why it is not possible to identify a single anomalous titre using the student's rule. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the uncertainty in a single titre and explain why the spread of 0.20 cm³ could be due to measurement uncertainty alone. [2]
  3. (c) Suggest what the student should do, and calculate the mean of the three titres. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 25.15 and 25.25 agree within 0.10, but so do 25.25 and 25.35; there are two possible concordant pairs and no clear outlier.
    B1 two pairs; B1 conclusion.
  2. (b) ±0.10 cm³ (two readings); two titres could differ by up to 0.20 cm³ from reading uncertainty alone, so none is clearly anomalous.
    B1 ±0.10; B1 reasoning.
  3. (c) Do another titration (or two) to see which values are concordant; mean of all three = 25.25 cm³.
    B1.

Q139Planning: variables & hypothesis · Core · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

A student plans to investigate how the number of carbon atoms in an alcohol molecule affects its enthalpy change of combustion, using methanol to pentan-1-ol.

  1. (a) Identify the independent variable, the dependent variable and two controlled variables. [4]
  2. (b) Write a hypothesis and give a reason. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) IV: number of carbon atoms (chain length); DV: ΔHc (from temperature rise, mass of fuel); CVs (any two): mass/volume of water, distance from flame to can, same calorimeter, same starting temperature, same temperature rise / same draught shielding.
    B1 IV; B1 DV; B1 each CV.
  2. (b) ΔHc becomes more negative by a roughly constant amount per extra CH₂, because each extra CH₂ adds the same bonds (C–C and two C–H) broken and forms one more CO₂ and H₂O.
    B1 trend; B1 reason.

Q142Planning: variables & hypothesis · Standard · Paper 5 style · 6 marks
  1. (a) A student plans to find how the solubility of potassium nitrate in water depends on temperature. State the independent and dependent variables and how the dependent variable will be measured. [3]
  2. (b) Predict how solubility changes with temperature and give a reason, given that dissolving KNO₃ is endothermic. [2]
  3. (c) Give one variable that must be kept constant. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Independent: temperature. Dependent: solubility (mass of KNO₃ dissolving in 100 g of water). Method: dissolve a known mass in a known mass of hot water, cool while stirring and record the temperature at which crystals first appear.
    B1 independent; B1 dependent with units; B1 measurement method.
  2. (b) Solubility increases with temperature: by Le Chatelier's principle, raising the temperature shifts the endothermic dissolving equilibrium to the right.
    B1 increases; B1 reason.
  3. (c) Mass (volume) of water / stirring rate / purity of salt.
    B1.
Q143Planning: variables & hypothesis · Standard · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

1-chlorobutane, 1-bromobutane and 1-iodobutane are hydrolysed by warming with aqueous silver nitrate in ethanol, and the time for a precipitate to appear is measured.

  1. (a) Identify the independent variable, the dependent variable and two controlled variables. [3]
  2. (b) Write a hypothesis, including the expected order of the times, and explain it. [3]
Show answer
  1. (a) IV: halogen in the halogenoalkane; DV: time for precipitate to appear; CVs: volume/concentration of AgNO₃, amount of halogenoalkane (drops), temperature (same water bath).
    B1 IV; B1 DV; B1 two CVs.
  2. (b) Iodobutane fastest (shortest time), then bromobutane, then chlorobutane slowest; C–I is the weakest bond (lowest bond enthalpy), so it breaks most easily, outweighing the greater polarity of C–Cl.
    B1 order; B1 bond enthalpy; B1 bond strength not polarity decides.
Q144Planning: variables & hypothesis · Standard · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

Pink [Co(H₂O)₆]²⁺ reacts with chloride ions: [Co(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 4Cl⁻ ⇌ [CoCl₄]²⁻ + 6H₂O, ΔH positive. [CoCl₄]²⁻ is blue. A student plans to investigate the effect of temperature on the position of this equilibrium.

  1. (a) Write a hypothesis and explain it. [2]
  2. (b) State the independent and dependent variables and suggest how the dependent variable can be measured quantitatively. [3]
  3. (c) Give two variables that must be kept constant. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) As temperature increases, the solution becomes more blue (more [CoCl₄]²⁻): the forward reaction is endothermic, so equilibrium shifts to the right to absorb heat.
    B1 prediction; B1 reason.
  2. (b) IV: temperature; DV: the amount/proportion of [CoCl₄]²⁻; measure the absorbance with a colorimeter (with a filter that blue [CoCl₄]²⁻ absorbs), after the sample has reached the bath temperature.
    B1 IV; B1 DV; B1 colorimeter.
  3. (c) Concentrations of cobalt(II) and chloride (same mixture/volume); same cuvette path length.
    B1 both.
Q145Planning: variables & hypothesis · Standard · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

A student's hypothesis is that the enthalpy change of neutralisation is the same for any strong acid with any strong alkali. The plan is to mix 25.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol/dm³ acid with 25.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol/dm³ alkali in a polystyrene cup. Expected ΔH = −57.1 kJ/mol; c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹; assume 50.0 g of solution.

  1. (a) Suggest suitable acid–alkali pairs to test the hypothesis. [1]
  2. (b) State the independent and dependent variables and two controlled variables. [3]
  3. (c) Calculate the temperature rise expected if the hypothesis is correct. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) e.g. HCl + NaOH, HNO₃ + NaOH, HCl + KOH, HNO₃ + KOH (monobasic strong acids, strong alkalis).
    B1.
  2. (b) IV: acid–alkali pair; DV: temperature rise; CVs: concentrations, volumes, starting temperature, same cup/lid.
    B1 IV; B1 DV; B1 CVs.
  3. (c) 6.83 °C
    M1 q = 0.0250 × 57 100 = 1428 J; A1 ÷ (50.0 × 4.18).
Q146Planning: variables & hypothesis · Standard · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

A student predicts that the pH of pure water falls as it is heated, because H₂O ⇌ H⁺ + OH⁻ is endothermic.

  1. (a) Explain why the hypothesis is reasonable. [2]
  2. (b) State the variables and how pH should be measured. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why the water is still neutral at the higher temperature even though its pH is below 7. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Increasing temperature shifts the endothermic equilibrium to the right, so Kw and [H⁺] increase and pH = −lg[H⁺] falls.
    B1 shift right; B1 [H⁺] rises → pH falls.
  2. (b) IV: temperature; DV: pH, measured with a pH meter (with temperature compensation, calibrated with buffers).
    B1 variables; B1 pH meter.
  3. (c) [H⁺] still equals [OH⁻].
    B1.
Q147Planning: variables & hypothesis · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

A student's hypothesis is: 'The rate of the thiosulfate–acid reaction doubles for every 10 °C rise in temperature.' At 20 °C the cross disappears in 160 s.

  1. (a) Use the hypothesis to predict the times at 30, 40 and 50 °C. [1]
  2. (b) Explain why a graph of lg(1/t) against temperature would test the hypothesis, and predict its gradient. [3]
  3. (c) Suggest a suitable range and number of temperatures, with a reason. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 80 s, 40 s, 20 s
    B1.
  2. (b) If rate doubles each 10 °C, rate ∝ 2^(T/10), so lg(rate) = (lg 2/10)T + constant: a straight line of gradient 0.0301 per °C.
    M1 exponential relationship; M1 log gives straight line; A1 0.0301 per °C.
  3. (c) At least five temperatures, about 20–60 °C: enough points for a line, and above 60 °C times are too short to measure accurately.
    B1.
Q148Planning: variables & hypothesis · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 8 marks

A student wants to test the hypothesis that the solubility of the Group 2 hydroxides increases down the group from Mg(OH)₂ to Ba(OH)₂.

  1. (a) Identify the independent variable, the dependent variable and two controlled variables. [3]
  2. (b) Outline how the dependent variable could be measured for each hydroxide. [3]
  3. (c) Explain why the titration would be inaccurate for magnesium hydroxide and suggest an alternative. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) IV: Group 2 metal; DV: solubility (mol/dm³) of the hydroxide; CVs: temperature, time allowed to reach saturation, volume of water.
    B1 each type.
  2. (b) Shake excess solid with water at constant temperature until saturated; filter (dry apparatus); titrate a measured volume of the filtrate with standard HCl; calculate [OH⁻] and hence solubility.
    B1 saturated; B1 filter; B1 titrate.
  3. (c) Mg(OH)₂ is so slightly soluble that the titre would be tiny (very large % error); measure the pH of the saturated solution with a pH meter and calculate [OH⁻] instead.
    B1 tiny titre; B1 alternative.
Q149Planning: variables & hypothesis · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

A student plans to find how temperature affects the rate of reaction of magnesium with acid. The plan is: 'Run 1: 20 °C, 1.0 mol/dm³ HCl. Run 2: 30 °C, 1.5 mol/dm³ HCl. Run 3: 40 °C, 2.0 mol/dm³ HCl. Use a different length of magnesium ribbon each time.'

  1. (a) Identify the flaws in the plan. [3]
  2. (b) Rewrite the plan's variables correctly. [2]
  3. (c) Write a hypothesis with a reason based on collision theory. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Concentration of acid changes as well as temperature; length (mass) of Mg changes; only three temperatures; no repeats – so effects cannot be attributed to temperature alone.
    1 mark for each of three flaws.
  2. (b) IV: temperature only (at least five values); keep acid concentration and volume, Mg length/mass and surface (cleaned) constant; DV: time to dissolve (rate = 1/t).
    B1 IV; B1 controlled/DV.
  3. (c) Rate increases with temperature because more particles have energy ≥ Ea, so more frequent successful collisions.
    B1.
Q150Planning: variables & hypothesis · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

A student tests the hypothesis that a catalyst lowers the activation energy of a reaction. The time t for a fixed extent of reaction is measured at 20 °C and 40 °C, with and without catalyst.without catalyst: 200 s (20 °C), 50 s (40 °C)with catalyst: 40 s (20 °C), 20 s (40 °C)Assume k ∝ 1/t; ln(k₂/k₁) = (Ea/R)(1/T₁ − 1/T₂); R = 8.31 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.

  1. (a) Calculate Ea with and without the catalyst. [3]
  2. (b) State whether the results support the hypothesis. [1]
  3. (c) Suggest two improvements to make the conclusion more reliable. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) without: 52.8 kJ/mol; with: 26.4 kJ/mol
    M1 1/293 − 1/313 = 2.18 × 10⁻⁴; A1 each.
  2. (b) Yes – Ea with catalyst is about half the uncatalysed value.
    B1.
  3. (c) Use more temperatures (at least five) and find Ea from the gradient of ln(1/t) against 1/T; repeat each measurement; keep mass/surface area of catalyst constant.
    1 mark each.
Q151Percentage error & uncertainty · Core · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

Uncertainties: burette ±0.05 cm³ per reading; 25.0 cm³ pipette ±0.06 cm³; balance ±0.005 g per reading. Titre = 23.40 cm³ (two burette readings). Mass of solid = 1.25 g (two balance readings).

  1. (a) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the titre. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the volume delivered by the pipette. [1]
  3. (c) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the mass of solid weighed by difference. [1]
  4. (d) Calculate the total percentage uncertainty and state which measurement contributes most. [1]
  5. (e) Suggest how the uncertainty in the mass could be reduced. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 0.43 %
    B1 (2 × 0.05)/23.40 × 100.
  2. (b) 0.24 %
    B1 0.06/25.0 × 100.
  3. (c) 0.80 %
    B1 (2 × 0.005)/1.25 × 100.
  4. (d) 1.47 %; the mass
    B1.
  5. (e) Use a larger mass of solid (and make a larger volume of solution) or a balance reading to 0.001 g.
    B1.

Q158Percentage error & uncertainty · Standard · Paper 5 style · 4 marks

A student finds the Mr of a volatile liquid to be 59.3. The accepted value is 58.1. The student estimates the total percentage uncertainty from the apparatus as 1.5 %.

  1. (a) Calculate the percentage difference between the student's value and the accepted value. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the range of values consistent with the student's uncertainty. [1]
  3. (c) Explain whether the difference can be explained by the apparatus uncertainties alone. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 2.1 %
    B1.
  2. (b) 59.3 ± 0.89, i.e. 58.4 to 60.2
    B1.
  3. (c) No: the accepted value 58.1 lies outside the range (2.1 % > 1.5 %), so there must also be a systematic error (e.g. air in syringe, vapour not at oven temperature).
    B1 comparison; B1 systematic error.

Q161Percentage error & uncertainty · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

In a thermochemistry experiment a thermometer reading to ±0.5 °C is used. The initial temperature is 24.5 °C and the final temperature is 31.0 °C.

  1. (a) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in ΔT. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage uncertainty if a thermometer reading to ±0.1 °C were used instead. [1]
  3. (c) Alternatively, the student doubles the concentrations of both solutions so that ΔT becomes 13.0 °C with the original thermometer. Calculate the new percentage uncertainty and compare the two improvements. [2]
  4. (d) The student's value of ΔH differs from the data-book value by 12 %. Explain whether the difference can be explained by the thermometer uncertainty alone. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 15.4 %
    B1 (2 × 0.5)/6.5 × 100.
  2. (b) 3.1 %
    B1 0.2/6.5 × 100.
  3. (c) 7.7 %; the more precise thermometer gives the bigger reduction (3.1 % compared with 7.7 %)
    B1 value; B1 comparison.
  4. (d) Yes: 12 % is less than the 15.4 % uncertainty in ΔT, so it lies within the experimental uncertainty.
    B1.

Q163Percentage error & uncertainty · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 6 marks

Q164Percentage error & uncertainty · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 4 marks

For the equilibrium A + B ⇌ 2C, Kc = [C]² ÷ ([A][B]). The equilibrium concentrations have these percentage uncertainties: [A] 1.0 %; [B] 1.0 %; [C] 1.5 %. Kc is calculated as 4.20.

  1. (a) Explain why the uncertainty in [C] is counted twice. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in Kc and the absolute uncertainty. [2]
  3. (c) Another group obtains Kc = 4.50. State, with a reason, whether the two results agree within uncertainty. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) [C] is squared, so its percentage uncertainty is doubled.
    B1.
  2. (b) 5.0 %; ±0.21
    B1 5.0 %; B1 ±0.21.
  3. (c) No: 4.20 + 0.21 = 4.41 < 4.50, so the results differ by more than the uncertainty (unless the other group's uncertainty overlaps).
    B1.

Q166Organic practical techniques (reflux, filtration, purity) · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Ethanal (boiling point 21 °C) is made by adding acidified potassium dichromate(VI) dropwise to warm ethanol (boiling point 78 °C) and distilling the product as it forms.

  1. (a) Explain why the ethanal is distilled off immediately rather than heated under reflux. [2]
  2. (b) Suggest why the collecting flask is surrounded by ice. [1]
  3. (c) State the colour change in the reaction flask. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Removing it as it forms stops it being oxidised further to ethanoic acid; under reflux it would return to the oxidising agent.
    B1 prevents further oxidation; B1 to carboxylic acid.
  2. (b) Ethanal has a very low boiling point (21 °C); cooling condenses it and reduces loss by evaporation.
    B1.
  3. (c) Orange to green.
    B1.

Show answer
  1. (a) It reacts with (neutralises) the acid impurity, forming a water-soluble salt.
    B1.
  2. (b) CO₂ gas is produced and builds up pressure, which must be released.
    B1.
  3. (c) The upper (organic) layer; run off and discard the lower aqueous layer through the tap, then collect the upper layer.
    B1 upper; B1 method.

Q169Organic practical techniques (reflux, filtration, purity) · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Pure benzoic acid melts at 122 °C. A sample made by a student melts over the range 116–120 °C.

  1. (a) State and explain what the result shows about the sample. [2]
  2. (b) Describe how the melting point is measured. [2]

    B1 impure; B1 lower and range.

Q170Organic practical techniques (reflux, filtration, purity) · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

A student heats a mixture of butan-1-ol and acidified potassium dichromate(VI) under reflux.

  1. (a) Describe the reflux apparatus, including how water flows through the condenser. [2]
  2. (b) Give the purpose of the anti-bumping granules. [1]
  3. (c) Explain why the apparatus must not be sealed at the top. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Round-bottomed (pear-shaped) flask with a condenser fitted vertically; water enters at the bottom and leaves at the top; heated with a water bath/electric heater, open at the top.
    B1 vertical condenser on flask; B1 water in at bottom.
  2. (b) They give smooth boiling, preventing sudden violent boiling (bumping).
    B1.
  3. (c) Pressure would build up as the mixture is heated and could cause an explosion.
    B1.
Q171Organic practical techniques (reflux, filtration, purity) · Standard · Paper 3 style · 6 marks
  1. (a) Ethanol is to be oxidised completely to ethanoic acid using acidified potassium dichromate(VI). Explain why the mixture is heated under reflux. [2]
  2. (b) A crude solid organic product is purified by recrystallisation. Describe the steps. [3]
  3. (c) Describe how the purity of the solid can be checked. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Reflux lets the mixture be heated for a long time without losing volatile ethanol or ethanal; vapours condense and return to the flask, so oxidation goes to completion.
    B1 vapour condenses and returns / no loss of volatile substances; B1 allows complete oxidation to the acid.
  2. (b) Dissolve in the minimum volume of hot solvent; filter hot to remove insoluble impurities; cool so that crystals form (soluble impurities stay in solution); filter under reduced pressure (Buchner funnel), wash with a little cold solvent and dry.
    1 mark each for any three steps.
  3. (c) Measure the melting point: a pure solid melts sharply at the data-book value; impurities lower it and widen the range.
    B1.
Q172Organic practical techniques (reflux, filtration, purity) · Standard · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

Ethyl ethanoate is prepared by heating 10.0 g of ethanoic acid with excess ethanol and a little concentrated sulfuric acid. After purification, 9.24 g of ester is obtained.CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH ⇌ CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O (Mr: ethanoic acid 60.0; ethyl ethanoate 88.0)

  1. (a) Calculate the theoretical yield and the percentage yield. [3]
  2. (b) Give two reasons why the percentage yield is below 100 %. [2]
  3. (c) State the role of the concentrated sulfuric acid. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 14.7 g; 63.0 %
    M1 n = 0.167 mol; A1 14.7 g; A1 63.0 %.

Q181Graph interpretation & conclusions · Core · Paper 5 style · 3 marks

On the graph of volume of CO₂ against time, B's curve is steeper at the start and levels off after 60 s; A's curve levels off after 200 s. Both level off at 120 cm³.

  1. (a) State two conclusions that can be drawn from the graph. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why both curves level off. [1]
Show answer

  2. (b) The calcium carbonate has been used up (the reaction has stopped).
    B1.

Q196Planning: safety · Core · Paper 5 style · 5 marks
  1. (a) For each substance give its main hazard and one precaution:(i) concentrated sulfuric acid; (ii) ethanol being heated; (iii) bromine water; (iv) solid sodium hydroxide. [4]
  2. (b) Explain why 'wear a lab coat' alone is not an acceptable safety precaution in a planning answer. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) (i) corrosive — wear goggles and gloves, add acid slowly to water. (ii) highly flammable — heat with a water bath or electric heater, no naked flame. (iii) toxic/irritant vapour — use in a fume cupboard (gloves). (iv) corrosive — wear goggles and gloves, avoid contact with skin.
    B1 each hazard with a matching precaution.
  2. (b) It is a general precaution, not specific to the hazards of the chemicals used.
    B1.

Q202Planning: safety · Standard · Paper 5 style · 4 marks

Ethyl ethanoate is made by heating ethanol and ethanoic acid with a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid under reflux.

  2. (b) Explain why goggles are not enough as an answer to 'state a safety precaution'. [1]
Show answer

(a) Concentrated H₂SO₄ is corrosive – add carefully with a dropping pipette, wear gloves and goggles; ethanol/ethyl ethanoate are flammable – electric heater/water bath, no flames; ethanoic acid (glacial) is corrosive with irritating vapour – dispense in fume cupboard; apparatus under reflux must not be sealed.

Q203Planning: safety · Standard · Paper 3 style · 3 marks

A student heats a solid in a boiling tube and collects the gas over water using a delivery tube under an inverted measuring cylinder.

  1. (a) Explain the danger of 'suck-back' and how to avoid it. [2]
  2. (b) Suggest why the boiling tube should be clamped near the mouth and sloped slightly downward toward the mouth when heating a hydrated solid. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) As the tube cools the gas contracts and cold water is drawn into the hot tube, which may crack; remove the delivery tube from the water before stopping heating (or use a Bunsen valve/trap).
    B1 explanation; B1 prevention.
  2. (b) A downward slope lets condensed water drain towards the cool mouth; if it ran back down to the hot base it could crack the glass.
    B1.
Q204Planning: safety · Standard · Paper 5 style · 4 marks

  2. (b) Describe how the student can show that the air has been removed. [1]
  3. (c) Explain why the hydrogen flow is continued while the copper cools. [1]

  1. (a) To flush out the air; a mixture of hydrogen and air can explode when heated.

  2. (b) Collect a sample of the gas at the outlet in a test-tube and light it: it should burn quietly, not pop/squeak.
    B1.
  3. (c) To stop hot copper being re-oxidised by air entering the tube.
    B1.

A technician prepares solutions from concentrated ammonia (about 15 mol/dm³) and concentrated hydrochloric acid (about 12 mol/dm³).

Show answer

Q210Planning: safety · Challenge · Paper 5 style · 5 marks

A student's plan contains this safety section: 'Wear safety glasses. Be careful with chemicals. Do everything in a fume cupboard. Wash hands at the end.' The experiment is the titration of aqueous ammonia (0.5 mol/dm³) with dilute hydrochloric acid (0.5 mol/dm³).

  1. (a) Evaluate this safety section. [3]
  2. (b) Rewrite it as two specific precautions. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) It is vague and not linked to specific hazards ('be careful' is not a precaution); a fume cupboard is unnecessary for dilute solutions; it does not mention the real hazards: dilute ammonia and HCl are irritant to eyes/skin; it omits pipette safety (use a pipette filler, never mouth pipette).
    1 mark for each valid criticism (max 3).
  2. (b) Use a pipette filler to fill the pipette with aqueous ammonia (irritant); wear goggles because both solutions are irritant to eyes, and rinse spills from the skin with water.
    1 mark each.

Q214Rates experiment: recording & processing · Core · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

The loss in mass (CO₂ escaping) is recorded.time / s: 0, 30, 60, 90mass of CO₂ lost, lumps / g: 0.00, 0.18, 0.30, 0.38mass of CO₂ lost, powder / g: 0.00, 0.42, 0.66, 0.80CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂ (Mr of CO₂ = 44.0; CaCO₃ = 100.1)

  2. (b) Explain the difference in rate in terms of surface area and collisions between particles. [2]
  3. (c) Calculate the amount, in mol, of CO₂ lost from the lumps by 90 s. [1]
  4. (d) Calculate the mass of calcium carbonate that has reacted in the powder experiment by 90 s. [2]
Show answer

Q222Rates experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

A fixed, small amount of iodine is used in each experiment; the reaction is timed until the iodine is all used up (the colour change from starch). Therefore rate = [I₂]₀ ÷ t.Experiment | [propanone] / mol/dm³ | [H⁺] / mol/dm³ | [I₂]₀ / mol/dm³ | time / s1 | 0.40 | 0.20 | 0.0010 | 1202 | 0.80 | 0.20 | 0.0010 | 603 | 0.40 | 0.40 | 0.0010 | 604 | 0.40 | 0.20 | 0.0020 | 240

  1. (a) Calculate the rate of reaction for experiment 1, in mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹, to 3 significant figures. [1]
  2. (b) Deduce the order of reaction with respect to propanone, H⁺ and I₂, using all four experiments. Give a reason. [3]
  3. (c) Calculate the rate constant k, with units, for rate = k[propanone][H⁺]. [2]
Show answer

A clock reaction is timed at five temperatures. For the Arrhenius equation, ln k = ln A − Ea/(RT), with R = 8.314 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.

  1. (a) Calculate 1/T and ln(1/t) at 293 K and at 333 K. Give 1/T to 4 significant figures and ln(1/t) to 3 significant figures. [2]

Show answer

  2. (b) -6013 K (approximately -6012.9 K, negative)

Q224Rates experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 7 marks

Sodium thiosulfate reacts with hydrochloric acid. Rate is taken as 1/t, where t is the time for a cross to disappear.Experiment | [S₂O₃²⁻] / mol/dm³ | [H⁺] / mol/dm³ | time / s1 | 0.20 | 0.50 | 252 | 0.10 | 0.50 | 503 | 0.10 | 0.25 | 50

  1. (a) Deduce the order of reaction with respect to S₂O₃²⁻ and with respect to H⁺, giving your reasons from the data. [3]
  2. (b) Calculate the rate constant k, with units, for rate = k[S₂O₃²⁻]. Use the rate = 1/t. [2]
  3. (c) Predict the time for a cross to disappear when [S₂O₃²⁻] = 0.40 mol/dm³ and [H⁺] = 2.00 mol/dm³. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) S₂O₃²⁻: first order (halving [S₂O₃²⁻] doubles t, so the rate halves, exp 1 → 2). H⁺: zero order (halving [H⁺] has no effect on t, exp 2 → 3).
    B1 first order with respect to thiosulfate; B1 reason (t doubles when concentration halves); B1 zero order with respect to H⁺ (same t when [H⁺] changes).
  2. (b) 0.200 dm³ mol⁻¹ s⁻¹

  3. (c) 12.5 s
    M1 1/t = k[S₂O₃²⁻] (H⁺ has no effect) = 0.200 × 0.40 = 0.0800 s⁻¹; A1 t = 12.5 s.
Q225Rates experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

A student measures the rate of a gas-loss reaction as (mass of gas lost) ÷ (time). The mass lost is 0.240 g ± 0.005 g (balance reading). The time is 48.0 s ± 0.5 s (stopwatch and judgement of the end point).

  1. (a) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the mass lost and in the time. [2]

Show answer
  1. (a) mass: 2.1 %; time: 1.0 %
    B1 0.005/0.240 × 100 = 2.08 %; B1 0.5/48.0 × 100 = 1.04 %.
  2. (b) 3.1 % (rate = 5.00 × 10⁻³ g/s, with a range of about 4.84 to 5.16 × 10⁻³ g/s)
    M1 add the percentage uncertainties: 2.08 % + 1.04 %; A1 3.1 %, so the rate is 5.00 × 10⁻³ ± 0.16 × 10⁻³ g/s.
  3. (c) Repeat the experiment several times and take a mean, or use a colour sensor / light sensor to judge the end point instead of the eye.
    B1 repeat and average, or use a sensor for the end point (to reduce judgement error).

Q228Gas collection experiment: recording & processing · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

A student needs to collect four gases in the laboratory: hydrogen, carbon dioxide, ammonia and chlorine. Relevant properties: H₂ is insoluble in water and less dense than air; CO₂ is slightly soluble in water and denser than air; NH₃ is very soluble in water and less dense than air; Cl₂ is poisonous, denser than air and reacts with water.

  1. (a) For each gas, state a suitable collection method from: over water, upward delivery, downward delivery, or a gas syringe in a fume cupboard. Give the reason for each choice. [4]
Show answer
  1. (a) H₂: over water (insoluble). CO₂: downward delivery (denser than air; slightly soluble, so not over water). NH₃: upward delivery (less dense than air; very soluble, so not over water). Cl₂: downward delivery in a fume cupboard (denser than air; toxic; reacts with water).
    B1 each gas with a suitable method; B1 each gas with a valid reason (1 mark each, max 4).
Q229Gas collection experiment: recording & processing · Core · Paper 3 style · 3 marks

A gas syringe is connected to a flask through a delivery tube. Before a reaction is started the student wants to check that the apparatus has no leaks.

  1. (a) Describe a simple leak test for this apparatus. [2]
  2. (b) Explain how a leak would affect the calculated relative atomic mass of a metal. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Seal the outlet (close the tap or pinch the delivery tube) so that only the syringe is open; pull the plunger out to a mark and release it. It should stay in place (or move only very slowly) if there is no leak; if it springs back, there is a leak.
    B1 seal/close the outlet so only the syringe is open; B1 move the plunger and release it; if it returns or does not hold the position, there is a leak.
  2. (b) Less gas is collected, so the moles of gas are too low; the calculated Ar of the metal is too high.
    B1 less gas measured, so the calculated Ar is too high (since Ar = mass ÷ moles of gas).

The volume of gas collected in the inverted measuring cylinder is 50.0 cm³ (measured at the same level of water inside and outside). Assume that the molar volume at 101.3 kPa and 20 °C is 24.0 dm³/mol.

  1. (a) Explain why the water vapour pressure must be subtracted from the atmospheric pressure before the volume is corrected. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the volume of dry hydrogen at 101.3 kPa, in cm³, to 3 significant figures. [2]
  3. (c) A 0.0500 g sample of M gave the corrected volume of hydrogen. Calculate the relative atomic mass of M (assume a 1:1 ratio of M to H₂). [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) The gas in the cylinder is a mixture with water vapour, which exerts a pressure; the pressure of the dry hydrogen is therefore lower than atmospheric pressure, so the moles of H₂ are not given by the volume read at 101.3 kPa.

Q241Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Core · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

The highest temperature reached is 31.5 °C.Zn + CuSO₄ → ZnSO₄ + CuAssume the solution has density 1.00 g/cm³ and specific heat capacity 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹. Ar: Zn = 65.4.

  1. (a) Calculate the amount, in mol, of CuSO₄ and of zinc, and identify the limiting reagent. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the energy released, in J, to 3 significant figures. [1]
  3. (c) Calculate the enthalpy change of reaction, ΔH, in kJ/mol, with its sign, to 3 significant figures. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) CuSO₄: 0.0100 mol; zinc: 0.0107 mol; CuSO₄ is limiting
    M1 n(CuSO₄) = 0.0500 × 0.200 = 0.0100 mol and n(Zn) = 0.70/65.4 = 0.0107 mol; A1 CuSO₄ is limiting (zinc in excess).

  2. (b) 9.23 kJ/g
    B1 11.54/1.25 = 9.23 kJ/g.
  3. (c) Heat is lost to the surroundings (air and the can); some of the crisp does not burn completely (soot/unburnt food); the can absorbs heat.
    B1 any reason for heat loss to surroundings / absorbed by the can; B1 incomplete combustion of the sample.
Q249Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

50.0 cm³ of dilute acid at 21.0 °C is added to an excess of magnesium in a polystyrene cup and the mixture is stirred. Because the temperature falls after the reaction is complete, the temperature is recorded every minute after the reaction is over:time / min: 3, 4, 5, 6, 7temperature / °C: 34.6, 34.2, 33.8, 33.4, 33.0Assume the solution has a mass of 50.0 g and c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹.

  1. (a) Use a graph or extrapolation back to the time of mixing (time 0) to find the maximum temperature. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the temperature rise, and the energy released, in J. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why extrapolation is necessary. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) 35.8 °C
    M1 the cooling rate is 0.40 °C per minute, so extrapolate back 3 min from 34.6 °C; A1 35.8 °C.
  2. (b) ΔT = 14.8 °C; q = 3093 J
    B1 ΔT = 35.8 − 21.0 = 14.8 °C; B1 q = 50.0 × 4.18 × 14.8 = 3093 J.
  3. (c) Heat is lost to the surroundings after mixing, so the highest reading taken is below the true maximum; extrapolating the cooling line back to the time of mixing estimates the temperature with no heat loss.
    B1 heat is lost during the time of the measurements, so the true maximum is found by extrapolation back to the mixing time.
Q250Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Standard · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Two experiments neutralise 25.0 cm³ of 2.00 mol/dm³ HCl with 25.0 cm³ of 2.00 mol/dm³ NaOH (both at 20.0 °C), in polystyrene cups. Experiment A uses a lid; experiment B has no lid. Temperature rises: A = 12.5 °C; B = 11.6 °C. Assume c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹ and density 1.00 g/cm³.

  1. (a) Calculate the enthalpy change of neutralisation for experiment A and for experiment B, in kJ/mol. [2]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage by which the value for B is smaller than the value for A. [1]
  3. (c) Explain why the lid gives a more accurate value. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) A: -52.3 kJ/mol; B: -48.5 kJ/mol
    B1 A: −50.0 × 4.18 × 12.5 ÷ 0.0500 = -52.3 kJ/mol; B1 B: −50.0 × 4.18 × 11.6 ÷ 0.0500 = -48.5 kJ/mol.
  2. (b) 7.2 %
    B1 (52.25 − 48.49)/52.25 × 100 = 7.2 %.
  3. (c) The lid reduces heat lost to the surroundings (by evaporation and convection), so more of the heat released stays in the solution.
    B1 less heat lost to surroundings, so the temperature rise is not underestimated.
Q251Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Standard · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Ethanol (Mr 46.0; ΔcH = −1367 kJ/mol) is burned in a spirit burner to heat 200 g of water from 20.0 °C to 50.0 °C. The burner is only 25 % efficient.

  1. (a) Calculate the energy needed to heat the water, in kJ. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the energy that must be released by the ethanol, in kJ, to allow for the 25 % efficiency. [1]
  3. (c) Calculate the mass of ethanol that must be burned, in g, to 3 significant figures. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 25.08 kJ
    B1 200 × 4.18 × 30.0 = 25080 J = 25.08 kJ.
  2. (b) 100.3 kJ
    B1 25.08 ÷ 0.25 = 100.3 kJ.
  3. (c) 3.38 g
    M1 n = 100.3/1367 = 0.07339 mol; A1 mass = 0.07339 × 46.0 = 3.38 g.
Q252Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

In an experiment, 50.0 cm³ of solution (mass 50.0 g ± 0.5 g) is used. The initial temperature is 20.0 °C and the highest temperature is 32.5 °C. Each thermometer reading is accurate to ±0.1 °C. The enthalpy change is calculated as ΔH = −mcΔT ÷ n, with c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹ and n = 0.0500 mol (exact).

  1. (a) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the temperature rise ΔT (ΔT = 12.5 °C). [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the mass of solution, and the total percentage uncertainty in ΔH (add the percentage uncertainties). [2]
  3. (c) Hence give the enthalpy change with its uncertainty, in kJ/mol, to 3 significant figures. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 1.6 %
    B1 uncertainty in ΔT = ±0.2 °C (both readings) ; 0.2/12.5 × 100 = 1.6 %.
  2. (b) mass: 1.0 %; total: 2.6 %
    B1 0.5/50.0 × 100 = 1.0 %; B1 total = 1.6 + 1.0 = 2.6 %.
  3. (c) -52.3 ± 1.4 kJ/mol
    M1 ΔH = −52.25 kJ/mol (from ΔT = 12.5 and mass 50.0 g); A1 uncertainty = 1.36 kJ/mol, so −52.3 ± 1.4 kJ/mol.
Q253Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

Two neutralisation experiments are compared. Each uses 25.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol/dm³ acid and 25.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol/dm³ NaOH (c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹, density 1.00 g/cm³). With ethanoic acid the temperature rise is 6.5 °C.Accepted value for strong acid neutralisation: H⁺(aq) + OH⁻(aq) → H₂O(l), ΔH = −57.3 kJ/molCH₃COOH(aq) + OH⁻(aq) → CH₃COO⁻(aq) + H₂O(l)

  1. (a) Calculate the enthalpy change of neutralisation for ethanoic acid, in kJ/mol, from the experiment. [2]
  2. (b) Use a cycle (CH₃COOH → H⁺ + CH₃COO⁻, then H⁺ + OH⁻ → H₂O) to calculate the enthalpy change of ionisation of ethanoic acid, CH₃COOH(aq) → CH₃COO⁻(aq) + H⁺(aq). [2]
  3. (c) Explain the sign of your answer to (b). [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) -54.3 kJ/mol
    M1 q = 50.0 × 4.18 × 6.5 = 1358.5 J and n = 0.0250 mol; A1 ΔH = −1358.5 ÷ 0.0250 = -54.3 kJ/mol.
  2. (b) +3.0 kJ/mol
    M1 ΔH(ionisation) = ΔH(experiment) − ΔH(H⁺ + OH⁻) = -54.3 − (−57.3); A1 +3.0 kJ/mol.
  3. (c) The ionisation is endothermic: energy is absorbed to break the O–H bond and separate the ions, so less heat is released in the neutralisation.
    B1 endothermic; energy needed to ionise the weak acid.
Q254Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

3.20 g of anhydrous copper(II) sulfate (Mr 159.6) is added to 50.0 cm³ of water at 20.0 °C in a polystyrene cup. The temperature rises to 26.3 °C.CuSO₄(s) → Cu²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq)

  1. (a) Calculate the amount, in mol, of CuSO₄ used. [1]
  2. (b) Calculate the enthalpy change of solution, ΔH, in kJ/mol, with its sign. [2]
  3. (c) State whether the process is exothermic or endothermic, and suggest why the value for the hydrated salt (CuSO₄·5H₂O) would be different. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) 0.02005 mol
    B1 3.20/159.6 = 0.02005 mol.
  2. (b) -65.7 kJ/mol
    M1 q = 50.0 × 4.18 × 6.3 = 1316.7 J; A1 ΔH = −1.3167 ÷ 0.02005 = -65.7 kJ/mol.
  3. (c) Exothermic (temperature rises). The hydrated salt has a different enthalpy of solution, because its Cu²⁺ ions are already surrounded by water of crystallisation in the solid; the lattice and hydration energy terms differ from those of the anhydrous salt, so the energy balance changes (the hydrated salt dissolves with a much smaller, endothermic enthalpy change).
    B1 exothermic (temperature rises); B1 the hydrate has a different enthalpy because its water of crystallisation changes the lattice and hydration energy balance.
Q255Thermochemistry experiment: recording & processing · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

25.0 cm³ of 2.00 mol/dm³ HCl and 25.0 cm³ of 2.00 mol/dm³ NaOH are mixed in a polystyrene cup, rise 12.5 °C (from 20.0 °C to 32.5 °C). The cup itself has a heat capacity of 25 J/K. Assume c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹ for the solution (mass 50.0 g) and 0.0500 mol of water is formed.

Q256Qualitative analysis: organic functional groups · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Compound A is a colourless organic liquid with the formula C₃H₆O. Results:Test 1: 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine (2,4-DNPH) reagent → orange precipitate.Test 2: warm with Tollens' reagent → no silver mirror.Test 3: warm with Fehling's solution → no red precipitate.

  1. (a) State the functional group shown by test 1, and which type of compound (aldehyde or ketone) A must be, using tests 2 and 3. [2]
  2. (b) Name compound A. [1]
  3. (c) Explain why Tollens' reagent does not react with a ketone. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) Carbonyl group, C=O; A is a ketone (no reaction with Tollens' or Fehling's, so not an aldehyde).
    B1 carbonyl group (C=O) shown by 2,4-DNPH; B1 ketone, since aldehydes give a silver mirror / red precipitate and ketones do not.
  2. (b) Propanone, CH₃COCH₃
    B1 propanone (the only ketone with formula C₃H₆O).
  3. (c) Ketones cannot be oxidised further under these conditions because the carbonyl carbon has no hydrogen atom attached to it.
    B1 ketones are not oxidised (no H on the C=O carbon); aldehydes are oxidised to carboxylic acids.
Q257Qualitative analysis: organic functional groups · Core · Paper 3 style · 4 marks

Three colourless compounds are shaken with aqueous bromine (orange) in a test tube, in the dark:Compound X: hexane — no change.Compound Y: cyclohexene — the orange colour is lost immediately.Compound Z: phenol — a white precipitate forms.

  1. (a) Name the functional group in compound Y that causes the change in colour. [1]
  2. (b) State the observation for compound X and explain it. [1]
  3. (c) Give the name of the white precipitate formed with compound Z, and explain why bromine substitutes three positions. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Carbon–carbon double bond (C=C), an alkene.
    B1 C=C (alkene).
  2. (b) No change, because hexane is a saturated alkane with no C=C bonds and does not react with bromine water.
    B1 no change; no C=C / saturated alkane.
  3. (c) 2,4,6-tribromophenol. The OH group activates the ring, so bromine substitutes at the positions 2, 4 and 6 (ortho and para to OH).
    B1 2,4,6-tribromophenol; B1 OH is electron-donating / activates the ring at 2, 4 and 6 positions.

Q262Qualitative analysis: organic functional groups · Standard · Paper 3 style · 5 marks

Four compounds are tested: propanal, propanone, butanone and propanoic acid. The results are:Test 1: Tollens' reagent, warm → silver mirror.Test 2: sodium carbonate solution → effervescence.Test 3: Fehling's solution, warm → red precipitate.

  2. (b) State which compound gives effervescence with sodium carbonate, and name the gas. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why propanal reduces Tollens' reagent, but propanone does not. [2]
Show answer
  1. (a) Propanal only.

  2. (b) Propanoic acid; carbon dioxide.

Q263Qualitative analysis: organic functional groups · Standard · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

Three halogenoalkanes are each warmed with aqueous sodium hydroxide for several minutes. The mixture is then acidified with dilute nitric acid and aqueous silver nitrate is added:1-chlorobutane: white precipitate, formed slowly.1-bromobutane: cream precipitate, formed faster.1-iodobutane: yellow precipitate, formed fastest.

  1. (a) Name the three precipitates, and write the ionic equation for the formation of the cream precipitate. [3]
  2. (b) Explain why the mixture must be acidified with dilute nitric acid before adding silver nitrate. [2]
  3. (c) Explain why the order of precipitation is I > Br > Cl. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) AgCl (white); AgBr (cream); AgI (yellow). Ag⁺(aq) + Br⁻(aq) → AgBr(s).
    B1 AgCl white, AgBr cream, AgI yellow (1 mark for all three correct, 2 for two); B1 Ag⁺ + Br⁻ → AgBr.
  2. (b) To remove the excess OH⁻ ions, which would react with silver ions to form a brown precipitate of silver(I) oxide, hiding the halide precipitate.
    B1 removes OH⁻ (excess alkali); B1 otherwise silver oxide (brown) forms and confuses the result.
  3. (c) The C–X bond strength decreases from C–Cl to C–I (C–I is weakest), so the iodoalkane hydrolyses fastest.
    B1 C–I bond is weakest (bond enthalpy C–Cl > C–Br > C–I), so it is broken fastest.

Q270Qualitative analysis: organic functional groups · Challenge · Paper 3 style · 6 marks

Two isomeric bromoalkanes, both C₄H₉Br, are each warmed with aqueous sodium hydroxide, then acidified with dilute nitric acid and treated with aqueous silver nitrate:compound Q (1-bromobutane): cream precipitate forms slowly.compound R (2-bromo-2-methylpropane): cream precipitate forms rapidly.

  1. (a) Explain why the reagent mixture is acidified before silver nitrate is added. [2]
  2. (b) Explain why compound R reacts faster than compound Q. [3]
  3. (c) Name the organic product formed by hydrolysis of compound R. [1]
Show answer
  1. (a) To remove excess OH⁻, which would precipitate silver(I) oxide (brown) and hide the bromide precipitate.
    B1 remove OH⁻ ions; B1 otherwise brown Ag₂O forms and the cream AgBr precipitate cannot be seen.
  2. (b) R is a tertiary halogenoalkane and hydrolyses by an SN1 mechanism through a relatively stable tertiary carbocation, whereas Q, a primary halogenoalkane, hydrolyses by SN2 with a less stable transition state; the carbocation route is faster.
    B1 R is tertiary; B1 SN1 via a stable tertiary carbocation (more stable than primary); B1 Q primary, SN2, slower.
  3. (c) 2-methylpropan-2-ol, (CH₃)₃COH
    B1 2-methylpropan-2-ol.
